b(s)-b(r)\ge-Cs.$$ Also $T(\mathbf 1_{B_r}F)=\mu(B_r)$, since $\left\langle Q,F\right\rangle_g=0$ almost everywhere. The bound $F-F_0=O(t)$ therefore implies $$\label{eq:density-error}
|b(r)-r^{-2}\mu(B_r(p))|\le Cr.$$

In particular $b$ is bounded. Fix a good radius $s$ in <a href="#eq:almost-monotone" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:almost-monotone">[eq:almost-monotone]</a> and let $r\downarrow0$ through good radii; then let $s\downarrow0$ along a sequence realizing the lower limit of $b$. The upper and lower limits agree. Equation <a href="#eq:density-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:density-error">[eq:density-error]</a> gives the density limit along good radii.

Only countably many spheres at a fixed center can have positive $\sigma$-mass. Any radius $r$ can therefore be bracketed by good radii in $((1-\varepsilon)r,r)$ and $(r,(1+\varepsilon)r)$. Ball inclusion, followed by $r\downarrow0$ and then $\varepsilon\downarrow0$, proves the asserted limit along all radii. Its finiteness follows from <a href="#eq:abstract-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:abstract-growth">[eq:abstract-growth]</a>. ◻

</details>

For $d_g(x,y)$ below the injectivity radius, set $z_x(y)=\exp_x^{-1}(y)$.

<a id="stmt-lem-transverse"></a>
<div id="lem:transverse" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:transverse"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Vanishing transverse moment). *Under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:splitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:splitting">13</a>, $$\label{eq:transverse}
\mathcal N_r:=
\int_{\mathscr C}\int_{B_r(x)}
\left|\operatorname{pr}_{L_\ell^\perp}\frac{z_x(y)}r\right|^2
\,d\mu(y)\,d\lambda(x,\ell)=o(r^2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-transverse"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transverse</summary>

<a id="eq:polarization-differentiation"></a>
*Proof.* Write $P=A\mu$ in a smooth local frame, so that $A(p)$ is the conditional average of the line bivectors over $p$. At $\mu$-almost every point, differentiation with respect to the Radon measure $\mu$ gives $$\label{eq:polarization-differentiation}
r^{-2}\int_{B_{Dr}(p)}|A(y)-A(p)|\,d\mu(y)\longrightarrow0
\qquad(D<\infty\text{ fixed}).$$ For the precise differentiation input one may use (Kinnunen 2026, Theorem 4.33 and Corollary 4.34) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Kinnunen2026](#ref-Kinnunen2026)]. To see the normalization, enclose $B_{Dr}(p)$ in a Euclidean coordinate ball of radius $CDr$. The mean oscillation on that ball tends to zero, while its mass divided by $r^2$ is bounded by <a href="#eq:abstract-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:abstract-growth">[eq:abstract-growth]</a>. This does not require $\mu$ to be doubling. Smooth changes of frame add a vanishing $O(r)$ error after this normalization.

<a id="eq:tangent-ball"></a>
Fix such a differentiation point $p$ with $\theta(p)>0$. The locally rescaled measures $$\nu_r=r^{-2}(z_p/r)_*\mu$$ have uniformly bounded mass on each compact subset of $T_pX$, so every sequence of scales has a vaguely convergent subsequence. Every limit $\nu$ satisfies $$\label{eq:tangent-ball}
\nu(B_D(0))=\theta(p)D^2\qquad(D>0).$$ Initially the identity follows at continuity radii of $\nu$. Increasing continuity radii recover the mass of each open ball, while decreasing continuity radii recover the corresponding closed ball. Both masses are $\theta(p)D^2$, so the identity holds for every $D>0$ and every centered sphere is $\nu$-null.

The current and measure rescalings have the same normalization. Indeed, put $D_r(y)=z_p(y)/r$. For a compactly supported test two-form $\beta=\sum_{i<j}\beta_{ij}(w)\,dw_i\wedge dw_j$ on $T_pX$, $$D_r^*\beta=r^{-2}\sum_{i<j}\beta_{ij}(z_p/r)\,dz_i\wedge dz_j.$$ Thus the pushforward $(D_r)_*T$, defined by testing $T$ against $D_r^*\beta$, already has the $r^{-2}$ factor in its coefficient measures; no further normalization is used. Equation <a href="#eq:polarization-differentiation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:polarization-differentiation">[eq:polarization-differentiation]</a> makes its $P$ part converge to $A(p)\nu$. Its $Q$ part vanishes in variation on each fixed ball, since <a id="eq:q-blowup"></a>$$\label{eq:q-blowup}
r^{-2}\int_{B_{Dr}(p)}|Q|\,dV_g
\le C_D\|Q\|_{L^2(B_{Dr}(p))}\longrightarrow0.$$ The last limit holds at every point by absolute continuity of the integral of $|Q|^2$. Compactly supported tests in the rescaled coordinates pull back inside the original coordinate chart for small $r$. Thus closure passes to the limit, and $A(p)\nu$ is a closed constant-polarization current on $T_pX$.

For a constant skew matrix $A$, this closure says $\sum_i A^{ij}\partial_i\nu=0$ for each $j$. Consequently $\partial_v\nu=0$ for every $v\in\operatorname{im}A(p)$. For a compactly supported smooth function $\phi$, the derivative of $t\mapsto\int\phi(w+tv)\,d\nu(w)$ is therefore zero. This proves that $\nu$ is invariant under translations along $\operatorname{im}A(p)$. The normalization $F_p(A(p))=1$ excludes rank zero. Rank four would imply that $\nu$ is a constant multiple of four-dimensional Lebesgue measure, contrary to <a href="#eq:tangent-ball" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tangent-ball">[eq:tangent-ball]</a>. Therefore $A(p)$ has real rank two.

The associated positive Hermitian matrix has trace one and complex rank one. Thus $A(p)$ is itself a unit complex line bivector. If $\lambda_p$ is the conditional probability measure on $\mathscr C_p$, then $$\int_{\mathscr C_p}|\ell-A(p)|^2\,d\lambda_p(\ell)
=1-|A(p)|^2=0.$$ Hence $\lambda_p$ is concentrated on $A(p)$. Denote its plane by $L$.

Translation invariance along $L$ implies $$\nu=\mathcal L^2_L\otimes\gamma$$ for a positive Radon measure $\gamma$ on $L^\perp$. For example, testing against a nonnegative compactly supported function in the normal variables gives a translation-invariant measure in the tangential variables, hence a multiple of area; these multiples define $\gamma$. Dividing the centered ball mass by $D^2$ in <a href="#eq:tangent-ball" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tangent-ball">[eq:tangent-ball]</a> yields <a id="eq:transverse-product"></a>$$\label{eq:transverse-product}
\theta(p)=\pi\int_{L^\perp}(1-|w|^2/D^2)_+\,d\gamma(w).$$ For $D_2>D_1$, the difference of these integrands is nonnegative and strictly positive at every $0<|w|<D_2$. Since the integrals are equal, $\gamma$ is supported at the origin. Every tangent limit is therefore $(\theta(p)/\pi)\mathcal L^2_L$.

It follows that the normalized inner integral in <a href="#eq:transverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transverse">[eq:transverse]</a> tends to zero at $\lambda$-almost every $(p,\ell)$ of positive density. Indeed, in the rescaled coordinates a continuous cutoff equal to one on $B_1$, supported in $B_2$, times $|\operatorname{pr}_{L^\perp}z|^2$ dominates the integrand and vanishes on every tangent limit. Subsequential compactness gives convergence along all scales.

When $\theta(p)=0$, the same conclusion follows directly by bounding the normalized inner integral by $r^{-2}\mu(B_r(p))$. These normalized integrals are uniformly bounded by <a href="#eq:abstract-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:abstract-growth">[eq:abstract-growth]</a>. Dominated convergence against the finite measure $\lambda$ proves <a href="#eq:transverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transverse">[eq:transverse]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:splitting"></a>
### Closedness of the density restriction

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-transverse-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transverse</summary>

<a id="eq:theta-volume"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:splitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:splitting">13</a>.* The existence of the density is Lemma <a href="#lem:density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:density">14</a>. We approximate the positive-density restriction by smooth functions of a regularized density. Closedness of $T$ then reduces the problem to controlling full derivatives against $Q$ and tangential derivatives against $P$. The tangential estimate combines the quantitative angular bound with the vanishing transverse moment from Lemma <a href="#lem:transverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transverse">15</a>; their product will make the cutoff boundary tend to zero. Choose a nonnegative, smooth, nonincreasing function $h:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)$, positive near zero and zero on a neighborhood of $[1,\infty)$. For $r$ below the injectivity radius define <a id="eq:density-cutoff"></a>$$\label{eq:density-cutoff}
f_r(x)=r^{-2}\int_Xh(d_g(x,y)^2/r^2)\,d\mu(y).$$ The kernel is smooth near the diagonal and vanishes before the cut locus, so $f_r$ is smooth. It is uniformly bounded. Writing $H(t)=h(t^2)$, integration of ball indicators gives $$f_r(x)=\int_0^1[-H'(t)]r^{-2}\mu(B_{rt}(x))\,dt
 \longrightarrow c_h\theta(x),
\qquad
c_h=\int_0^1h(u)\,du>0.$$ The quadratic growth bound supplies domination for this limit. In particular $\theta$ is Borel measurable. Differentiation of the finite measure $\mu$ with respect to four-dimensional volume also shows that $$\label{eq:theta-volume}
\theta(x)=0\quad\text{for volume-almost every }x.$$ Indeed, apply the differentiation theorem for Radon measures (Kinnunen 2026, Theorem 4.19) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Kinnunen2026](#ref-Kinnunen2026)] in a fixed coordinate chart, with smooth volume as the denominator measure. At volume-almost every center the measure-to-volume ratios of sufficiently small coordinate balls are bounded. Enclosing a geodesic ball of radius $r$ in a coordinate ball of comparable radius therefore gives $\mu(B_r(x))=O_x(r^4)$, and hence $r^{-2}\mu(B_r(x))\to0$.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The error from the $L^2$ correction

<a id="eq:q-cutoff"></a>
Put $M_r(x)=\mu(B_r(x))$. Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\begin{aligned}
r^{-3}\int_X|Q|M_r\,dV_g
&\le
\left(\int_X|Q|^2r^{-2}M_r\,dV_g\right)^{1/2}
\left(\int_Xr^{-4}M_r\,dV_g\right)^{1/2}
\longrightarrow0.
\label{eq:q-cutoff}
\end{aligned}$$ For the second factor, Fubini and the four-dimensional volume bound give $$\int_Xr^{-4}M_r(x)\,dV_g(x)
=r^{-4}\int_X\operatorname{vol}_g(B_r(y))\,d\mu(y)
\le C\mu(X).$$ For the first, $r^{-2}M_r$ is bounded and tends to zero volume-almost everywhere by <a href="#eq:theta-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-volume">[eq:theta-volume]</a>. Dominated convergence applies to $|Q|^2$.

<a id="eq:q-full-derivative"></a>
First variation gives $|df_r(x)|\le Cr^{-3}M_r(x)$. Thus <a href="#eq:q-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:q-cutoff">[eq:q-cutoff]</a> also proves $$\label{eq:q-full-derivative}
\int_X|Q|\,|df_r|\,dV_g\longrightarrow0.$$

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Tangential derivatives of the density cutoff

<a id="eq:tangential-cutoff"></a>
We prove the complementary estimate $$\label{eq:tangential-cutoff}
\int_{\mathscr C}|df_r(x)|_{L_\ell}\,d\lambda(x,\ell)
\longrightarrow0,$$ where the subscript means restriction of the covector to the two-plane $L_\ell$.

For $(x,\ell)\in\mathscr C$, $y\in B_r(x)$, and $m\in\mathscr C_y$, put $$z=z_x(y),\qquad z^\perp=\operatorname{pr}_{L_\ell^\perp}z,
\qquad \delta=|\ell-\tau_{yx}m|.$$ These quantities measure different errors (Figure <a href="#fig:cutoff-geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:cutoff-geometry">1</a>). Closedness will replace a derivative along $L_\ell$ by normal derivatives. Differentiating the radial kernel normally contributes $|z^\perp|/r$, while the resulting mixed two-forms contribute $\delta$. Keeping these factors together is what makes their integrated product overcome the cutoff’s $r^{-3}$ normalization.

<figure id="fig:cutoff-geometry">

<figcaption>The two factors in the tangential cutoff estimate. The left panel lies in the subspace spanned by $L_{ℓ}$ and the normal displacement. The right panel lies in the span of two unit bivectors, in a different ambient vector space. Closedness produces the product $δ|z^{⊥}|/r$. The squared integrals of its factors are $ℐ_{r} = O(r^{4})$ and $𝒩_{r} = o(r^{2})$, respectively.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-cutoff-geometry"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:cutoff-geometry`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
% definitions used by this figure, copied from the source preamble
\newcommand{\R}{\mathbb R}
\newcommand{\ip}[2]{\left\langle #1,#2\right\rangle}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth, line cap=round, line join=round,
                    font=\small, scale=0.95]
  \begin{scope}
    \node at (0.2,2.25) {Displacement in \(T_xX\)};
    \filldraw[fill=blue!7, draw=blue!35]
      (-1.6,-0.6) -- (1.5,-0.6) -- (2.15,0.2) -- (-0.95,0.2) -- cycle;
    \node[blue!60!black] at (-1.0,-0.37) {\(L_\ell\)};
    \coordinate (O) at (0,0);
    \coordinate (Zt) at (1.05,-0.12);
    \coordinate (Z) at (1.05,1.45);
    \draw[->,thick,blue!65!black] (O) -- (Zt);
    \draw[->,thick,black!80] (O) -- (Z)
      node[midway,left] {\(z/r\)};
    \draw[->,thick,red!65!black] (Zt) -- (Z)
      node[midway,right] {\(z^\perp/r\)};
    \fill (O) circle (1.4pt);
    \node[below left] at (O) {\(0\)};
    \fill (Zt) circle (1.2pt);
    \fill (Z) circle (1.2pt);
    \node at (0.2,-1.05) {\(L_\ell+\R z^\perp\subset T_xX\)};
  \end{scope}
  \begin{scope}[xshift=6.5cm]
    \node at (0.45,2.25) {Chord in \(\Lambda^2T_xX\)};
    \coordinate (O) at (0,0);
    \coordinate (A) at (1.6,0);
    \coordinate (B) at (65:1.6);
    \draw[densely dashed,black!30] (1.6,0) arc (0:78:1.6);
    \draw[->,thick,blue!65!black] (O) -- (A);
    \draw[->,thick,blue!65!black] (O) -- (B);
    \draw[thick,red!65!black] (A) -- (B)
      node[midway,right] {\(\delta\)};
    \node[below] at (A) {\(\ell\)};
    \node[above left] at (B) {\(\tau_{yx}m\)};
    \fill (O) circle (1.4pt);
    \node[below left] at (O) {\(0\)};
    \node at (0.45,-0.62) {\(|\ell|=|\tau_{yx}m|=1\)};
    \node at (0.45,-1.05) {\(1-\ip{\ell}{\tau_{yx}m}=\delta^2/2\)};
  \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 15 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Two geometric diagrams side by side illustrating error factors:
- Left panel titled "Displacement in $T_x X$": Shows a horizontal baseline representing the subspace $L_\ell$, origin $0$, a vector from $0$ to point $z/r$, a red dashed vertical perpendicular dropped from $z/r$ to $L_\ell$ labeled $z^\perp/r$, and a shaded blue region along the baseline labeled $L_\ell + \mathbb{R} z^\perp \subset T_x X$.
- Right panel titled "Chord in $\Lambda^2 T_x X$": Shows an origin $0$ with two unit bivector rays labeled $\tau_{yx} m$ and $\ell$ extending from $0$, connected at their tips by a chord labeled $\delta$ forming an isosceles triangle with a shaded blue base. Below the diagram are the equations $|\ell| = |\tau_{yx} m| = 1$ and $1 - \langle \ell, \tau_{yx} m \rangle = \delta^2/2$.
```


<a id="eq:center-derivative"></a>
Fix $(x,\ell)$. Choose orthonormal normal coordinates $z=z_x(y)$ with $\ell=e_1\wedge e_2$, and put $h_r(z)=h(|z|^2/r^2)$. At the fixed center $x$, first variation of squared distance gives $d_x[d_g(x,y)^2](e_i)=-2z_i$. Hence, for $i=1,2$, $$\label{eq:center-derivative}
df_r(x)e_i=-r^{-2}\int_X\partial_{z_i}h_r\,d\mu(y).$$ This differentiates the scalar distance kernel. We now freeze $x,\ell$, and these coordinates, and use closedness of $T$ in the $y$ variable.

<a id="eq:comparison-error"></a>
Coordinate covectors in normal coordinates differ from radially parallel covectors by $O(|z|^2)$. Indeed, the Jacobi equation along $t\mapsto\exp_x(tz)$, in a parallel frame, has curvature coefficient $O(|z|^2)$. The field with initial data $(0,v)$ is therefore $tv+O(|z|^2|v|)$ for $0\le t\le1$, giving $\tau_{yx}\circ d(\exp_x)_z=\mathrm{id}+O(|z|^2)$. Inverting gives the same estimate for coordinate covectors. Since both line bivectors have norm one, <a id="eq:quadratic-coordinate"></a>$$\label{eq:quadratic-coordinate}
(dz_1\wedge dz_2)(m)
=\left\langle \ell,\tau_{yx}m\right\rangle+O(r^2)
=1-\tfrac12\delta^2+O(r^2)\qquad(|z|<r).$$ All errors are uniform. Comparing <a href="#eq:center-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:center-derivative">[eq:center-derivative]</a> with the $P$ part of the current below, and bounding its $Q$ part, gives $$\begin{aligned}
\left|df_r(x)e_i+
r^{-2}T\bigl((\partial_{z_i}h_r)\,dz_1\wedge dz_2\bigr)\right|
&\le Cr^{-3}\int_{d_g(x,y)<r}(\delta^2+r^2)
                    \,d\lambda(y,m)\nonumber\\
&\quad+Cr^{-3}\int_{B_r(x)}|Q|\,dV_g.
\label{eq:comparison-error}
\end{aligned}$$ After integration against $\lambda(x,\ell)$, its first term is $O(r)$ by <a href="#eq:abstract-angular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:abstract-angular">[eq:abstract-angular]</a>. Its $r^2$ term is $O(r)$ by <a href="#eq:abstract-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:abstract-growth">[eq:abstract-growth]</a>. Its last term tends to zero by Fubini and <a href="#eq:q-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:q-cutoff">[eq:q-cutoff]</a>. Thus the integrated comparison error vanishes.

For $i=1$, apply closure to $d(h_rdz_2)$; for $i=2$, apply it to $d(h_rdz_1)$. These one-forms are compactly supported in the chart and extend smoothly by zero. Since $d(dz_j)=0$, each $T\bigl((\partial_{z_i}h_r)\,dz_1\wedge dz_2\bigr)$ is, up to sign, a sum of terms $$T\bigl((\partial_{z_a}h_r)\,dz_a\wedge dz_j\bigr),
\qquad a=3,4,\quad j\in\{1,2\}.$$ For the normal derivatives, $$|\partial_{z_a}h_r|
\le Cr^{-1}\left|\operatorname{pr}_{L_\ell^\perp}(z/r)\right|.$$ The mixed coordinate two-forms satisfy $$|(dz_a\wedge dz_j)(m)|\le C(\delta+r^2).$$ Indeed their radially parallel counterparts vanish on $\ell$, and their coordinate error is $O(r^2)$.

The integrated $Q$ and $r^2$ errors are controlled as in <a href="#eq:comparison-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-error">[eq:comparison-error]</a>. Including the outer factor $r^{-2}$, the remaining term is at most $$Cr^{-3}\iint_{d_g(x,y)<r}
\delta\left|\operatorname{pr}_{L_\ell^\perp}(z_x(y)/r)\right|
\,d\lambda(y,m)\,d\lambda(x,\ell).$$ By Cauchy–Schwarz, <a href="#eq:abstract-angular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:abstract-angular">[eq:abstract-angular]</a>, and Lemma <a href="#lem:transverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:transverse">15</a>, this is bounded by <a id="eq:critical-scaling"></a>$$\label{eq:critical-scaling}
Cr^{-3}\sqrt{\mathcal I_r}\sqrt{\mathcal N_r}
=r^{-3}O(r^2)o(r)=o(1).$$ The second squared integral is exactly $\mathcal N_r$, since its integrand does not depend on $m$. Combining with <a href="#eq:comparison-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-error">[eq:comparison-error]</a> proves <a href="#eq:tangential-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tangential-cutoff">[eq:tangential-cutoff]</a>. No derivative of a center-dependent frame occurs: first variation was applied before the fixed-chart current tests.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Passing to the positive-density set

<a id="eq:weighted-current-limit"></a>
Let $b:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$ be smooth with $b(0)=0$. On the uniformly bounded range of $f_r$, both $b$ and $b'$ are bounded. Dominated convergence gives $$\label{eq:weighted-current-limit}
b(f_r)T\longrightarrow b(c_h\theta)P$$ as currents. For $P$ this follows from pointwise convergence of $f_r$. For $Q$ it follows from <a href="#eq:theta-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:theta-volume">[eq:theta-volume]</a> and $Q\in L^1$.

If $\eta$ is a smooth one-form, closedness of $T$ and the product rule give $$(b(f_r)T)(d\eta)
=-T\bigl(b'(f_r)\,df_r\wedge\eta\bigr).$$ The $Q$ contribution tends to zero by <a href="#eq:q-full-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:q-full-derivative">[eq:q-full-derivative]</a>. A line bivector $\ell$ evaluates $df_r\wedge\eta$ using only the restriction of $df_r$ to $L_\ell$, so the $P$ contribution tends to zero by <a href="#eq:tangential-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tangential-cutoff">[eq:tangential-cutoff]</a>. It follows from <a href="#eq:weighted-current-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-current-limit">[eq:weighted-current-limit]</a> that $b(c_h\theta)P$ is closed.

Finally take $b_n(s)=1-e^{-ns}$. On the relevant nonnegative range, $0\le b_n\le1$ and $b_n(c_h\theta)\to\mathbf 1_E$. A second dominated-convergence limit proves that $P_E=\mathbf 1_EP$ is closed. The limits are successive: first $r\downarrow0$ for each fixed $n$, then $n\to\infty$. No bound on $b_n'$ uniform in $n$ is required. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:conclusion"></a>
### The final pairing

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-transverse-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-transverse</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Let $\omega$ be a smooth closed form taming $J$. Suppose for contradiction that there is no compatible symplectic form, and take $P,Q,T,\lambda,\mu$ from Proposition <a href="#prop:separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:separation">5</a>. Propositions <a href="#prop:growth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:growth">6</a> and <a href="#prop:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:energy">12</a> verify all the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:splitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:splitting">13</a>. Consequently the current $P_E=\mathbf 1_{\{\theta>0\}}P$ is closed. Set $$P'=\mathbf 1_{X\setminus E}P,\qquad
T'=P'+Q=T-P_E.$$ Then $T'$ is closed. The current $P'$ is represented by the restriction $\lambda'\le\lambda$ to base points outside $E$, with projection $\mu'=\mathbf 1_{X\setminus E}\mu$. We pair $T$ with this residual current rather than with itself: placing the second positive factor on zero-density centers makes the previously bounded negative error in the pairing tend to zero.

<a id="eq:liminf"></a>
We first show that the negative term in <a href="#eq:positive-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-pairing">[eq:positive-pairing]</a> tends to zero for this choice of $P'$. For each $y\notin E$, <a id="eq:zero-density-tail"></a>$$\label{eq:zero-density-tail}
r^{-2}\int_X(1+d_g(x,y)/r)^{-6}\,d\mu(x)
\longrightarrow0.$$ On the inner ball and on every fixed dyadic shell this follows from $s^{-2}\mu(B_s(y))\to0$. The quadratic mass bound dominates the shell contributions by $C2^{-4j}$, a summable sequence, giving the full limit. The uniform bound <a href="#eq:shell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell">[eq:shell]</a> then permits dominated convergence against $\mu'$. Discarding the nonnegative angular term in <a href="#eq:positive-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-pairing">[eq:positive-pairing]</a> yields $$\label{eq:liminf}
\liminf_{r\downarrow0}\left\langle S_rP,*S_rP'\right\rangle_{L^2}\ge0.$$

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:zero-pairing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:zero-pairing">8</a> to $T'$. Its regularizations are in $L^2$, since $P'$ has measure coefficients and $Q\in L^2$. Expanding gives $$0=\left\langle S_rP,*S_rP'\right\rangle
+\left\langle S_rP,*S_rQ\right\rangle
+\left\langle S_rQ,*S_rP'\right\rangle
+\|S_rQ\|_2^2.$$ Both mixed terms tend to zero by <a href="#eq:cross-vanish" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cross-vanish">[eq:cross-vanish]</a>, applied once to $P$ and once to $P'$. Since $S_rQ\to Q$ strongly in $L^2$, <a href="#eq:liminf" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:liminf">[eq:liminf]</a> implies $0\ge\|Q\|_2^2$. Therefore $Q=0$.

It follows that $P=T$ annihilates all smooth closed forms. In particular $P(\omega)=0$. On the other hand, $\omega_x(\ell)>0$ on the compact bundle $\mathscr C$; it has a positive minimum there. Equation <a href="#eq:positive-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:positive-representation">[eq:positive-representation]</a> and $\lambda(\mathscr C)=1$ give $P(\omega)>0$, a contradiction.

The positive cone and the subspace of closed invariant smooth forms therefore intersect. Any form in their intersection is the required smooth compatible symplectic form for $J$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:cones"></a>
### The compatible cone

The distinction between existence and realization of a specified class appears in the separating functional. For existence, it annihilates all closed invariant forms. For a specified class, it need only annihilate exact invariant forms. We first establish the estimates in this setting, then identify the classes of the resulting currents.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Invariant representatives and separation

We retain the Hermitian metric, the projection $R$, and the closed lift $B=dd^*G+H$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:lift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:lift">4</a>. For a closed current $D$, let $\mathfrak c(D)\in H^2(X;\mathbb R)$ denote its intersection-dual class: $$D(\alpha)=\mathfrak c(D)\cdot[\alpha]
 \quad\text{for every smooth closed two-form }\alpha.$$ Equivalently, $\mathfrak c(D)$ is represented by the Hodge star of the harmonic part of the distributional two-form associated with $D$.

<a id="stmt-lem-invariant-cohomology"></a>
<div id="lem:invariant-cohomology" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16**. *The classes represented by closed invariant forms are exactly $\mathcal V=(H_J^-)^\perp$. The intersection form is positive definite on $H_J^-$, and $$H^2(X;\mathbb R)=\mathcal V\oplus H_J^-.$$ The operator $1-BR$ takes exact forms to exact invariant forms.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-invariant-cohomology"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-invariant-cohomology</summary>

*Proof.* Closed anti-invariant forms are self-dual, hence harmonic. Their intersection pairing is their $L^2$ inner product, so it is positive definite on their classes. Invariant and anti-invariant forms wedge to zero pointwise.

For any closed two-form $\alpha$, the form $(1-BR)\alpha$ is closed and invariant, and $$[\alpha]=[(1-BR)\alpha]+[HR\alpha],$$ because $BR\alpha=dd^*GR\alpha+HR\alpha$. The second summand is represented by a closed anti-invariant form. The two subspaces are orthogonal, and positive definiteness on the second makes their intersection zero. This proves the decomposition and the assertion about $\mathcal V$.

If $\alpha$ is exact, it is $L^2$-orthogonal to every harmonic form. For a harmonic anti-invariant form $\beta$, $\left\langle R\alpha,\beta\right\rangle_{L^2}=\left\langle \alpha,\beta\right\rangle_{L^2}=0$. Thus $HR\alpha=0$, and both $BR\alpha$ and $(1-BR)\alpha$ are exact. ◻

</details>

A current is called *positive* here if it annihilates anti-invariant forms and is nonnegative on pointwise semipositive invariant forms. As in Proposition <a href="#prop:separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:separation">5</a>, it has a representation by a finite positive measure on $\mathscr C$. Its trace measure is the projection to $X$.

<a id="stmt-lem-class-separation"></a>
<div id="lem:class-separation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:class-separator-sign"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Prescribed-class separation). *Let $a\in\mathcal V$ have no compatible representative. There are currents $P,Q,T\in H^{-3}$, with $P$ nonzero and positive, such that <a id="eq:class-exact-tests"></a><a id="eq:class-current"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 P(F)=1,\qquad P\text{ annihilates exact invariant forms},
 \label{eq:class-exact-tests}\\
 T=P+Q\text{ is closed},\qquad RQ=Q=*Q,
 \label{eq:class-current}\\
 \mathfrak c(T)\in\mathcal V,\qquad
 \mathfrak c(T)\cdot a\le0.
 \label{eq:class-separator-sign}
\end{gathered}$$ Moreover, a class $b\in H^2(X;\mathbb R)$ is taming if and only if $$b\cdot\mathfrak c(N)>0
 \quad\text{for every nonzero closed positive current }N.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-class-separation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-class-separation</summary>

*Proof.* By Lemma <a href="#lem:invariant-cohomology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:invariant-cohomology">16</a>, the closed invariant representatives of $a$ form a nonempty affine space. Separate this affine space from the open cone of positive invariant forms in the Fréchet space of smooth invariant forms. Hahn–Banach separation gives a nonzero continuous functional, nonnegative on the positive cone and nonpositive on the affine space. It vanishes on the direction space, which consists of exact invariant forms. Indeed, translating by any real multiple of a direction preserves the affine space, while positive scalings preserve the cone. Extend the functional by $1-R$ and normalize it as in Proposition <a href="#prop:separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:separation">5</a> to obtain $P(F)=1$. The same positivity argument gives its measure representation.

Define $T(\alpha)=P((1-BR)\alpha)$ and $Q(\alpha)=-P(BR\alpha)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:invariant-cohomology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:invariant-cohomology">16</a> shows that $T$ annihilates every exact form, so it is closed. The projection identities give $RQ=Q=*Q$. A closed anti-invariant form $\beta$ lies in the kernel of the operator used to construct $G$; hence $B\beta=\beta$ and $T(\beta)=0$. Thus $\mathfrak c(T)\in\mathcal V$. On a closed invariant representative of $a$, the currents $T$ and $P$ agree, proving <a href="#eq:class-separator-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:class-separator-sign">[eq:class-separator-sign]</a>. The Sobolev assertion follows from the measure representation and the order-zero lift, as in Proposition <a href="#prop:separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:separation">5</a>.

For the last statement, a taming representative evaluates strictly positively on every nonzero positive current. Conversely, if $b$ has no taming representative, separate its affine space of closed representatives from the open cone of forms positive on complex lines. The separator annihilates all exact forms. Since arbitrary anti-invariant forms can be added to that cone, it also annihilates all anti-invariant forms. It is therefore a nonzero closed positive current $N$, and $b\cdot\mathfrak c(N)\le0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Pairings for currents of arbitrary class

<a id="stmt-lem-cohomological-pairing"></a>
<div id="lem:cohomological-pairing" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cohomological-pairing"></a>
**Lemma 18**. *For closed currents $D_1,D_2\in H^{-3}$, $$\label{eq:cohomological-pairing}
 \left\langle S_rD_1,*S_rD_2\right\rangle_{L^2}
 =\mathfrak c(D_1)\cdot\mathfrak c(D_2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cohomological-pairing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cohomological-pairing</summary>

*Proof.* The forms $*S_rD_i$ lie in $H^3$, are distributionally closed, and have the harmonic components representing $\mathfrak c(D_i)$. Here we use commutation with $d^*,*$, and the fact that $S_r$ is the identity on harmonic forms. Their heat regularizations are smooth closed representatives of these classes. The intersection identity for smooth forms passes to the limit in $L^2$. In degree two on a four-manifold, $\alpha\wedge\beta=(*\alpha)\wedge(*\beta)$, giving <a href="#eq:cohomological-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cohomological-pairing">[eq:cohomological-pairing]</a>. ◻

</details>

We record the estimates that apply to the separator in Lemma <a href="#lem:class-separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:class-separation">17</a>. In the next proposition, positive currents are not required to have any prescribed cohomology class.

<a id="stmt-prop-class-estimates"></a>
<div id="prop:class-estimates" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:class-growth"></a>
<a id="eq:class-angular"></a>
**Proposition 19**. *Suppose that $P\ne0$ is positive, annihilates exact invariant forms, and $T=P+Q$ is closed, where $Q\in H^{-3}$ is anti-invariant. Write $\lambda$ for a positive measure representing $P$, and $\mu$ for its trace. Then $$\begin{gathered}
 \mu(B_s(x))\le Cs^2,\qquad Q\in L^2,
 \label{eq:class-growth}\\
 \iint_{d_g(x,y)<r}|\ell-\tau_{yx}m|^2
       \,d\lambda(x,\ell)\,d\lambda(y,m)\le Cr^4.
 \label{eq:class-angular}
\end{gathered}$$ Consequently the positive-density restriction $P_E$ is closed, where $E=\{x:\lim_{s\downarrow0}s^{-2}\mu(B_s(x))>0\}$.*

<a id="eq:class-mixed-limit"></a>
*If $A$ is any positive current whose trace has quadratic growth, then $$\label{eq:class-mixed-limit}
 \lim_{r\downarrow0}\left\langle S_rA,*S_rQ\right\rangle_{L^2}=0.$$ Every closed positive current has quadratic trace growth.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-class-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-class-estimates</summary>

*Proof.* Each radial test $Du=(1-BR)dd_J^cu$ in Section <a href="#sec:mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:mass">3</a> is exact by Lemma <a href="#lem:invariant-cohomology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:invariant-cohomology">16</a>. Its pointwise estimates therefore give the quadratic growth bound under the present annihilation hypothesis. The normalization $P(F)=1$ is inessential: for arbitrary finite mass it only changes the constant. In particular, this argument applies to every closed positive current.

<a id="eq:general-smoothed-mass"></a>
<a id="eq:general-positive-pairing"></a>
We will use the kernel estimates for any pair $A_1,A_2$ of positive currents with quadratic-growth trace measures $\mu_1,\mu_2$. Write $\lambda_1,\lambda_2$ for their representing measures. The proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:positive-pairing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:positive-pairing">10</a>, using Lemma <a href="#lem:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:kernel">9</a> and <a href="#eq:angular-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:angular-identity">[eq:angular-identity]</a>, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \|S_rA_i\|_2&\le C_i r^{-1},\label{eq:general-smoothed-mass}\\
 \left\langle S_rA_1,*S_rA_2\right\rangle_{L^2}
 &\ge cr^{-4}\iint_{d<r}|\ell-\tau_{yx}m|^2
            \,d\lambda_1(x,\ell)\,d\lambda_2(y,m)
 \nonumber\\
 &\quad-Cr^{-2}\iint(1+d/r)^{-6}
                     \,d\mu_1(x)\,d\mu_2(y),
 \label{eq:general-positive-pairing}
\end{aligned}$$ where $d=d_g(x,y)$. These statements do not require either measure to be dominated by the other. The shell bound <a href="#eq:shell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell">[eq:shell]</a> holds separately for each $\mu_i$, proving the norm bound and a uniform bound on the negative term.

<a id="eq:general-mixed-bound"></a>
For any anti-invariant distribution $W$ with $S_rW\in L^2$, Lemma <a href="#lem:mixing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:mixing">11</a> and <a href="#eq:general-smoothed-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:general-smoothed-mass">[eq:general-smoothed-mass]</a> give $$\label{eq:general-mixed-bound}
 |\left\langle S_rA_i,*S_rW\right\rangle_{L^2}|
 \le Cr\|S_rA_i\|_2\|S_rW\|_2
 \le C_i'\|S_rW\|_2.$$ Apply <a href="#eq:cohomological-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cohomological-pairing">[eq:cohomological-pairing]</a> to $T,T$. Since $Q$ is self-dual, expansion and these estimates yield $$cr^{-4}\mathcal I_r+\|S_rQ\|_2^2
 \le C+|\mathfrak c(T)^2|+C\|S_rQ\|_2,$$ where $\mathcal I_r$ is the angular integral in <a href="#eq:class-angular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:class-angular">[eq:class-angular]</a>. Hence $S_rQ$ is uniformly bounded in $L^2$, and the angular bound follows. Weak compactness and distributional convergence identify an $L^2$ limit with $Q$.

For smooth anti-invariant $W$, $$\left\langle S_rA,*S_rW\right\rangle=A(S_r^2W)\longrightarrow A(W)=0.$$ The uniform bound <a href="#eq:general-mixed-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:general-mixed-bound">[eq:general-mixed-bound]</a>, the $L^2$ contraction property of $S_r$, and approximation by smooth anti-invariant forms give <a href="#eq:class-mixed-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:class-mixed-limit">[eq:class-mixed-limit]</a>. Finally, <a href="#eq:class-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:class-growth">[eq:class-growth]</a>–<a href="#eq:class-angular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:class-angular">[eq:class-angular]</a> verify the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:splitting" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:splitting">13</a>, which proves that $P_E$ is closed. ◻

</details>

The next lemma permits pairing the zero-density part with positive currents unrelated to the original separator.

<a id="stmt-lem-general-residual"></a>
<div id="lem:general-residual" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:general-residual"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Residual pairings). *Let $A,P'$ be positive currents whose trace measures $\nu,\mu'$ have quadratic growth. Suppose $$\lim_{s\downarrow0}s^{-2}\mu'(B_s(x))=0
 \quad\text{for }\mu'\text{-almost every }x.$$ Then $$\label{eq:general-residual}
 \liminf_{r\downarrow0}\left\langle S_rA,*S_rP'\right\rangle_{L^2}\ge0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-general-residual"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-general-residual</summary>

<a id="eq:cross-small-density"></a>
*Proof.* We first show $$\label{eq:cross-small-density}
 (\mu'\times\nu)\{(x,y):d_g(x,y)<s\}=o(s^2).$$ For small $s$, choose an $s$-separated maximal set of centers and let $B_j$ be their radius-$s$ balls. They cover $X$, and their fixed dilates have uniformly bounded overlap, by the volume comparison in smooth coordinate charts. If $x\in B_j$ and $d_g(x,y)<s$, then $y\in2B_j$. Thus $$(\mu'\times\nu)\{d<s\}
 \le\sum_j\mu'(B_j)\nu(2B_j).$$ Bounded overlap gives $$\sum_j\mu'(B_j)^2
 \le C\int_X\mu'(B_{2s}(x))\,d\mu'(x)=o(s^2).$$ Indeed, the integrand divided by $s^2$ is bounded by quadratic growth and tends to zero at $\mu'$-almost every center. Also $$\sum_j\nu(2B_j)^2
 \le Cs^2\sum_j\nu(2B_j)=O(s^2).$$ Cauchy–Schwarz proves <a href="#eq:cross-small-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cross-small-density">[eq:cross-small-density]</a>.

In <a href="#eq:general-positive-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:general-positive-pairing">[eq:general-positive-pairing]</a>, discard the angular term. The remaining negative error tends to zero: on $d<r$, and on each fixed shell $2^jr\le d<2^{j+1}r$, this follows from <a href="#eq:cross-small-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cross-small-density">[eq:cross-small-density]</a>. Quadratic growth bounds the shell contributions by $C2^{-4j}$, allowing summation by dominated convergence. This proves <a href="#eq:general-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:general-residual">[eq:general-residual]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Proof of the cone description

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-general-residual-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-general-residual</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:taming-cones" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:taming-cones">2</a>.* Adding a closed anti-invariant form does not change positivity on complex lines. Lemma <a href="#lem:invariant-cohomology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:invariant-cohomology">16</a> therefore gives <a href="#eq:tame-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tame-decomposition">[eq:tame-decomposition]</a>. In particular $\mathcal C$ is nonempty. It is a convex cone of positive-square classes in $\mathcal V$, since its classes have closed taming representatives.

A compatible form wedges strictly positively with every taming form. Indeed, in a unitary frame diagonalizing the compatible form, the wedge product is a positive linear combination of the two positive diagonal entries of the taming form’s invariant part. Consequently every $a\in\mathcal K_J^c$ lies in $\mathcal C$ and pairs positively with every class in $\mathcal C$, hence nonnegatively with $\overline{\mathcal C}$.

The compatible cone is open in $\mathcal V$: add sufficiently small linear combinations of closed invariant representatives of a basis of $\mathcal V$ to a compatible form. The intersection form on $\mathcal V$ is nondegenerate by Lemma <a href="#lem:invariant-cohomology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:invariant-cohomology">16</a>. If $a\cdot y=0$ for a nonzero $y\in\overline{\mathcal C}$, a sufficiently small perturbation of $a$ within the compatible cone would pair negatively with $y$. Thus every compatible class satisfies the strict positivity in <a href="#eq:compatible-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compatible-duality">[eq:compatible-duality]</a>.

Conversely, suppose $a\in\mathcal C$ has this strict positivity but no compatible representative. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:class-separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:class-separation">17</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:class-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:class-estimates">19</a>. Let $$P'=P-P_E,\qquad T'=P'+Q=T-P_E,\qquad
 y=\mathfrak c(T').$$ Both $P_E$ and $T'$ are closed. Since $P_E$ is invariant, $\mathfrak c(P_E)\in\mathcal V$, and hence $y\in\mathcal V$. The trace $\mu'$ of $P'$ satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:general-residual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:general-residual">20</a>: it is bounded by $\mu$ and is concentrated where the latter has zero two-density.

We claim that $y\in\overline{\mathcal C}$. Let $N$ be any nonzero closed positive current. Its trace has quadratic growth by Proposition <a href="#prop:class-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:class-estimates">19</a>. By <a href="#eq:cohomological-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cohomological-pairing">[eq:cohomological-pairing]</a>, $$y\cdot\mathfrak c(N)
 =\left\langle S_rN,*S_rP'\right\rangle+\left\langle S_rN,*S_rQ\right\rangle.$$ The mixed term tends to zero by <a href="#eq:class-mixed-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:class-mixed-limit">[eq:class-mixed-limit]</a>, and Lemma <a href="#lem:general-residual" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:general-residual">20</a> bounds the lower limit of the first term by zero. Thus $y\cdot\mathfrak c(N)\ge0$. Since $a$ is taming, $(y+\varepsilon a)\cdot\mathfrak c(N)>0$ for every $\varepsilon>0$. The last part of Lemma <a href="#lem:class-separation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:class-separation">17</a> shows that $y+\varepsilon a$ is taming. It also lies in $\mathcal V$, so $y+\varepsilon a\in\mathcal C$, proving the claim.

It follows that $a\cdot y\ge0$, with strict inequality if $y\ne0$. If $P'\ne0$, then $y\ne0$. To see this, suppose $y=0$ and pair $T'$ with itself using <a href="#eq:cohomological-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cohomological-pairing">[eq:cohomological-pairing]</a>. We obtain $$0=\left\langle S_rP',*S_rP'\right\rangle+2\left\langle S_rP',*S_rQ\right\rangle+\|S_rQ\|_2^2.$$ The residual lemma, the mixed-term limit, and strong $L^2$ convergence of $S_rQ$ force $Q=0$. Then the nonzero positive current $P'=T'$ evaluates strictly positively on a closed tamer, contrary to $\mathfrak c(T')=0$.

Finally, a taming representative of $a$ gives $a\cdot\mathfrak c(P_E)\ge0$, strictly if $P_E\ne0$. At least one of $P_E,P'$ is nonzero. Therefore $$a\cdot\mathfrak c(T)
 =a\cdot\mathfrak c(P_E)+a\cdot y>0,$$ contradicting <a href="#eq:class-separator-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:class-separator-sign">[eq:class-separator-sign]</a>. This proves <a href="#eq:compatible-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compatible-duality">[eq:compatible-duality]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-general-residual-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-general-residual</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:taming-cones" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:taming-cones">3</a>.* The subspace $H_J^-$ is positive definite, while its orthogonal complement $\mathcal V$ contains the nonempty positive-square cone $\mathcal C$. Hence $h_J^-\le b_2^+-1$. If equality holds, the intersection form on $\mathcal V$ has signature $(1,b_2^-)$. Convexity puts $\mathcal C$ in one component of its positive-square cone. Every element of that component pairs strictly positively with every nonzero element of its closure, by Cauchy–Schwarz in coordinates of signature $(+,-,\ldots,-)$. Thus all $a\in\mathcal C$ satisfy <a href="#eq:compatible-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compatible-duality">[eq:compatible-duality]</a>, and $\mathcal C=\mathcal K_J^c$. Equation <a href="#eq:tame-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tame-decomposition">[eq:tame-decomposition]</a> gives the asserted identity. If $b_2^+=1$, the inequality forces $h_J^-=0$, proving the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ex-strict-cones"></a>
<div id="ex:strict-cones" class="example">

*Example 21* (Strict inclusion on the four-torus). On $X=\mathbb R^4/\mathbb Z^4$, use the Euclidean metric and standard orientation, and put $e^{ij}=dx_i\wedge dx_j$. Set $$U=e^{12}+e^{34},\qquad V_0=e^{13}-e^{24},\qquad
 W=e^{14}+e^{23},$$ $$f=\tfrac14\sin(2\pi x_1),\qquad
 k=\tfrac14\sin(2\pi x_2),\qquad
 r=(1+f^2+k^2)^{1/2}.$$ The form $F=(U+fV_0+kW)/r$ is self-dual and has squared norm two, so it is the fundamental form of a smooth orthogonal almost complex structure $J$ of this orientation. The form $$\eta=U+2f e^{13}+2k e^{23}$$ is closed. Its self-dual part is $rF$, so it is invariant, and its anti-self-dual part is $$f(e^{13}+e^{24})+k(e^{23}-e^{14}).$$ Its Hermitian eigenvalues are $r\pm\sqrt{f^2+k^2}>0$. Thus $\eta$ is compatible. The sine terms are exact, giving $[\eta]=[U]$.

A closed anti-invariant form is self-dual and harmonic for the flat metric, hence is a constant combination $aU+bV_0+cW$. Orthogonality to $F$ gives $a+bf+ck=0$ everywhere. Varying $x_1,x_2$ yields $a=b=c=0$; thus $H_J^-=0$. The closed forms $U\pm V_0$ have invariant projections $(1\pm f)F/r$, so both tame $J$. Their classes pair to zero, since $U^2=V_0^2$. Neither class can have a compatible representative, which would wedge strictly positively with a tamer in the other class. Hence $$\mathcal K_J^c+H_J^-=\mathcal K_J^c
 \subsetneq\mathcal K_J^t.$$ In Theorem <a href="#thm:taming-cones" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:taming-cones">2</a>, the class $[U+V_0]$ fails the strict positivity test against $[U-V_0]\in\mathcal C$, and conversely.

</div>

    {
      "caption": "The two factors in the tangential cutoff estimate. The left panel lies in the subspace spanned by $L_{ℓ}$ and the normal displacement. The right panel lies in the span of two unit bivectors, in a different ambient vector space. Closedness produces the product $δ|z^{⊥}|/r$. The squared integrals of its factors are $ℐ_{r} = O(r^{4})$ and $𝒩_{r} = o(r^{2})$, respectively.",
      "figure_id": "fig:cutoff-geometry",
      "pdf_page": 15,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:cutoff-geometry",
          "exact_quote": "The two factors in the tangential cutoff estimate.\nThe left panel lies in the subspace spanned by \\(L_\\ell\\) and\nthe normal displacement. The right panel lies in the span of\ntwo unit bivectors, in a different ambient vector space.\nClosednes",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=stealth, line cap=round, line join=round,\n                    font=\\small, scale=0.95]\n  \\begin{scope}\n    \\node at (0.2,2.25) {Displacement in \\(T_xX\\)};\n    \\filldraw[fill=blue!7, draw=blue!35]\n      (-1.6,-0.6) -- (1.5,-0.6) -- (2.15,0.2) -- (-0.95,0.2) -- cycle;\n    \\node[blue!60!black] at (-1.0,-0.37) {\\(L_\\ell\\)};\n    \\coordinate (O) at (0,0);\n    \\coordinate (Zt) at (1.05,-0.12);\n    \\coordinate (Z) at (1.05,1.45);\n    \\draw[->,thick,blue!65!black] (O) -- (Zt);\n    \\draw[->,thick,black!80] (O) -- (Z)\n      node[midway,left] {\\(z/r\\)};\n    \\draw[->,thick,red!65!black] (Zt) -- (Z)\n      node[midway,right] {\\(z^\\perp/r\\)};\n    \\fill (O) circle (1.4pt);\n    \\node[below left] at (O) {\\(0\\)};\n    \\fill (Zt) circle (1.2pt);\n    \\fill (Z) circle (1.2pt);\n    \\node at (0.2,-1.05) {\\(L_\\ell+\\R z^\\perp\\subset T_xX\\)};\n  \\end{scope}\n  \\begin{scope}[xshift=6.5cm]\n    \\node at (0.45,2.25) {Chord in \\(\\Lambda^2T_xX\\)};\n    \\coordinate (O) at (0,0);\n    \\coordinate (A) at (1.6,0);\n    \\coordinate (B) at (65:1.6);\n    \\draw[densely dashed,black!30] (1.6,0) arc (0:78:1.6);\n    \\draw[->,thick,blue!65!black] (O) -- (A);\n    \\draw[->,thick,blue!65!black] (O) -- (B);\n    \\draw[thick,red!65!black] (A) -- (B)\n      node[midway,right] {\\(\\delta\\)};\n    \\node[below] at (A) {\\(\\ell\\)};\n    \\node[above left] at (B) {\\(\\tau_{yx}m\\)};\n    \\fill (O) circle (1.4pt);\n    \\node[below left] at (O) {\\(0\\)};\n    \\node at (0.45,-0.62) {\\(|\\ell|=|\\tau_{yx}m|=1\\)};\n    \\node at (0.45,-1.05) {\\(1-\\ip{\\ell}{\\tau_{yx}m}=\\delta^2/2\\)};\n  \\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two geometric diagrams side by side illustrating error factors:\n- Left panel titled \"Displacement in $T_x X$\": Shows a horizontal baseline representing the subspace $L_\\ell$, origin $0$, a vector from $0$ to point $z/r$, a red dashed vertical perpendicular dropped from $z/r$ to $L_\\ell$ labeled $z^\\perp/r$, and a shaded blue region along the baseline labeled $L_\\ell + \\mathbb{R} z^\\perp \\subset T_x X$.\n- Right panel titled \"Chord in $\\Lambda^2 T_x X$\": Shows an origin $0$ with two unit bivector rays labeled $\\tau_{yx} m$ and $\\ell$ extending from $0$, connected at their tips by a chord labeled $\\delta$ forming an isosceles triangle with a shaded blue base. Below the diagram are the equations $|\\ell| = |\\tau_{yx} m| = 1$ and $1 - \\langle \\ell, \\tau_{yx} m \\rangle = \\delta^2/2$."
    }

{

      "basis": "inferred",

      "explanation": "Both works analyze geometric structures on four-manifolds by separating positive closed currents from obstructed cones and controlling the mass scaling of the resulting measures.",
      "relation": "shared_technique",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-prop-separation",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-342.1",
      "source_quote": "If $J$ admits no compatible symplectic form, there are currents",
      "target_anchor": "thm:main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-341.1",
      "target_quote": "Let $X$ be a closed connected oriented smooth four-manifold",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "directed",
      "explanation": "The taming cone equality applies to general four manifolds when the anti invariant dimension equals the positive index minus one which directly holds for the twistor family on these manifolds.",
      "relation": "generalises",
      "source_anchor": "cor:taming-cones",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-342.1",
      "source_quote": "Under the same hypotheses, write $h_J^-=\\dim H_J^-$ and let $b_2^+(X)$ be the positive index of the intersection form.",
      "target_anchor": "stmt-lem-twistor-degree",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-341.1",
      "target_quote": "The closed $J_v$-anti-invariant forms form the two-dimensional space $\\mathcal A_v$.",

    }
